ANNEXES

Chapter 15
Neglecting Correlation in Higher-Order Calculations

Here, we motivate our widely used approximation for neglecting the correlation between
G
and
\int\differential{W_t}
. This approximation is used, for instance, in calculating the mean squared error, taking a form such as:

A=\mathbb{E}\left[{G^{-1}_{i,j}G^{-1}_{i,k}\int_{0}^{\tau}b_{i}(x_{t})dW_{t}\int_{0}^{\tau}b_{j}(x_{s})dW_{s}}\right]\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}_{i,j}\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}_{i,k}\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{\tau}b_{i}(x_{t})b_{j}(x_{t})\differential{t}}\right]

(221)

To motivate this approximation, we treat, as an example, an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process in one dimension:

\differential{x_t}=ax_{t}\differential{t}+\differential{W_t}

(222)

where
W_{t}
is a Wiener process. The significant advantage of this very simple model is that an exact analytical solution exists:

x_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-a(t-s)}\differential{W_s}

(223)

with
x_{0}=0
. If we use a simple basis
\mathcal{B}=\{x\}
, the Gram matrix is a scalar and is equal to

G=\int_{0}^{\tau}x_{t}^{2}\differential{t}=\int_{0}^{\tau}\left(\int_{0}^{t}e^{-a(t-s)}\differential{W_s}\right)^{2}\differential{t}

(224)

We are going to express
G
as a sum of its average and its fluctuating part. To do so, we need to write:

\displaystyle\differential{\left(x_t^2e^{2at}\right)}=2\int_{0}^{t}e^{a(s+t)}\differential{W_s}\differential{W_t}+e^{2at}\differential{t}

(225)

\displaystyle\implies x_{\tau}^{2}e^{2a\tau}=\frac{e^{2a\tau}-1}{2a}+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}e^{a(s+t)}\differential{W_{s}}\differential{W_t}

(226)

\displaystyle\implies x_{\tau}^{2}=\frac{1-e^{-2a\tau}}{2a}+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-a(2\tau-(s+t))}\differential{W_{s}}\differential{W_t}

(227)

where we used
x_{0}=0
and, for two functions
u,v
:
d(uv)=v\differential{u}+u\differential{v}+\differential{u}\differential{v}
with the convention that
\differential{W_t}^{2}=\differential{t}
. Another possible way to obtain this result is to split the integral
\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential{W_s}\differential{W_t}
into two parts: one where
t=s
and one where
t\neq s
. Thus,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{1-e^{-2at}}{2a}\differential{t}+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}e^{-a(2t-(s^{\prime}+s))}\differential{W_{s'}}\differential{W_s}\differential{t}

(228)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{2a}-\frac{1}{4a^{2}}\left(1-e^{-2a\tau}\right)+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}\int_{s}^{\tau}e^{-a(2t-(s^{\prime}+s))}\differential{t}\differential{W_{s'}}\differential{W_s}

(229)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{\tau}{2a}-\frac{1}{4a^{2}}\left(1-e^{-2a\tau}\right)}_{\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{1}{a}e^{a(s+s^{\prime})}\left(e^{-2as}-e^{-2a\tau}\right)\differential{W_{s'}}\differential{W_s}}_{I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]}

(230)

We also obtain that
\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]\right)^{2}}\right]=\frac{\tau}{2a^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(1)
. Thus, for large
\tau
, the fluctuations of
I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]^{2}
become negligible with respect to
\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]
. Hence, we write that
G^{-1}\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-2}I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]
. We also use the fact that:

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}x_{s}\differential{W_s}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=G+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}x_{s}x_{s^{\prime}}\differential{W_s'}\differential{W_s}

(231)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]+I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]+2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}x_{s}x_{s^{\prime}}\differential{W_s'}\differential{W_s}

(232)

To go deeper into the calculation, we need to take into account the fluctuations of
x_{s}x_{s^{\prime}}
with
s>s^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle x_{s}x_{s^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2a}\left(e^{-a(s-s^{\prime})}-e^{-a(s+s^{\prime})}\right)+\left(2\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{s^{\prime\prime}}+\int_{s^{\prime}}^{s}\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}\right)e^{-a(s+s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime\prime\prime})}\differential{W_{s'''}}\differential{W_{s''}}

(233)

Thus, we can write:

\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}x_{s}\differential{W_s}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]+I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]+I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]+I_{(1,1,1,1)}[f_{3}]

(234)

where

\displaystyle I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a}\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}\left(e^{-a(s-s^{\prime})}-e^{-a(s+s^{\prime})}\right)\differential{W_s'}\differential{W_s}

(235)

\displaystyle I_{(1,1,1,1)}[f_{3}]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}\left(2\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{s^{\prime\prime}}+\int_{s^{\prime}}^{s}\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}\right)e^{-a(s+s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime\prime\prime})}\differential{W_{s'''}}\differential{W_{s''}}\differential{W_{s'}}\differential{W_{s}}

(236)

Given this simplification, we are able to calculate:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-2}I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]\right)^{2}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]+I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]+I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]+I_{(1,1,1,1)}[f_{3}]\right)}\right]

(237)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}-\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-3}\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]\left(I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]+2I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]\right)}\right]

(238)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]\left(I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]+2I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]\right)}\right]\right)

(239)

Furthermore, given the structure of
I_{(1,1)}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{s}f_{1}^{2}\differential{s'}\differential{s}

(240)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{2a^{3}}-\frac{5}{8a^{4}}+\frac{4{\left(2a\tau+1\right)}e^{-2a\tau}+e^{-4a\tau}}{8a^{4}}

(241)

Similarly, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1,1)}[f_{1}]I_{(1,1)}[f_{2}]}\right]=\frac{{\left(2\,a^{2}\tau^{2}+4\,a\tau+{\left(2\,a\tau-3\right)}e^{\left(2\,a\tau\right)}+3\right)}e^{\left(-2\,a\tau\right)}}{4\,a^{4}}

(242)

Finally, we obtain:

A=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-2}\frac{3\tau}{2a^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-2})\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}\left(1-\mathcal{O}(\tau^{-1})\right)

(243)

Hence, we have shown that our approximation, where
G
is treated as independent of
\int_{0}^{\tau}b_{i}(x_{t})\differential{W_t}\int_{0}^{\tau}b_{i}(x_{s})\differential{W_s}
, holds up to a factor of
\frac{1}{\tau}
for the case of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process in one dimension. This result could be generalized to a multi-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. But can we generalize it to any other type of process? Certainly not. For instance, consider a pure Brownian process (
\differential{x_t}=\differential{W_t}
); in this case,
x_{t}=\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential{W_s}
. Thus,
x_{t}
depends equally on all previous realizations of
\differential{W_s}
. If we try to fit this process with a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{x\}
, the Gram matrix will be
G=\frac{\tau}{2}+I_{(1,1)}[2(\tau-s)]
. Its fluctuations are
\mathbb{E}\left[{4I_{(1,1)}^{2}}\right]=\frac{\tau^{4}}{8}
, which are of the same order of magnitude as
\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]
. Thus, it would not be possible to write
E[G^{-1}]\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1}+o(\mathbb{E}\left[{G}\right]^{-1})
and, by extension, to neglect any type of cross-correlation. I think that the key ingredient for neglecting the cross-correlation is that the drift
f
confines the trajectory to a finite region of space. More precisely, this implies the existence of attractors. Hence, each kick received by the particle from the dynamical noise will be dampened as the trajectory converges to the attractor position. Unfortunately, it is unclear how to derive a general demonstration without linearizing the original SDE to match the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck case.

Chapter 16
Bias and Mean Squared Error for large
\Delta t

This section provides a detailed derivation of the bias and Mean Squared Error (MSE) for estimators of the drift parameters
{\alpha}
. While similar to the estimators discussed in the main text, the versions analyzed here are evaluated under the simplification that
4{\bar{D}}={I}
, where
{I}
is the identity matrix.

We consider the SDE system in one dimension:

\differential{x_t}=\underbrace{f(x_{t})}_{\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}{b}_{k}(x_{t})}\differential{t}+\underbrace{g(x_{t})}_{\sqrt{2D(x_{t})}}\differential{W_t}

Under this condition, we evaluate the following estimators:

•

The
Approximate Maximum Likelihood (AML) estimator
, defined using the matrix
G_{ij}=\langle{b}_{i}(x_{t})\cdot{b}_{j}(x_{t})\rangle
:

\hat{\alpha}_{i}=({G}^{-1})_{ij}\left\langle\frac{{x}_{t+\Delta t}-{x}_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot{b}_{j}(x_{t})\right\rangle

(244)

•

The
Trapeze estimator
, defined using the matrix
G^{Tr}_{ij}
:

\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{(Tr)}=(({G}^{Tr})^{-1})_{ij}\left\langle\frac{{x}_{t+\Delta t}-{x}_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot{b}_{j}(x_{t})\right\rangle

(245)

with
G^{Tr}_{ij}=\left\langle\frac{{b}_{j}(x_{t})+{b}_{j}({x}_{t+\Delta t})}{2}\cdot{b}_{i}(x_{t})\right\rangle
,

We now proceed to derive the bias and MSE for these two estimators.

Appendix 16.A
For the Approximate Maximum Likelihood (AML) estimator

We define the following integrals:

\displaystyle I_{(0),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\differential{s}=\Delta t

(246)

\displaystyle I_{(1),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\differential{W_t}

(247)

\displaystyle I_{(0,1),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{s_{1}}\differential{W_{s_1}}\differential{s_2}

(248)

\displaystyle I_{(1,0),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{s_{1}}\differential{s_1}\differential{W_{s_2}}

(249)

\displaystyle I_{(0,0),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{s_{1}}\differential{s_1}\differential{s_2}=\Delta t^{2}/2

(250)

\displaystyle I_{(1,1),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{s_{1}}\differential{W_{s_1}}\differential{W_{s_2}}

(251)

\displaystyle I_{(1,1,1),t}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{s_{1}}\int_{t}^{s_{2}}\differential{W_{s_1}}\differential{W_{s_2}}\differential{W_{s_3}}

(252)

Then, from
[
60
, p. 182]
, we can write the Taylor expansion of
x_{t+\Delta t}
as:

x_{t+\Delta t}=x_{t}+fI_{(0),t}+gI_{(1),t}+L_{0}fI_{(0,0),t}+L_{0}gI_{(0,1),t}\\
+L_{1}fI_{(1,0),t}+L_{1}gI_{(1,1),t}+L_{1}L_{1}gI_{(1,1,1),t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{2})

(253)

where
f=f(x_{t})
,
g=g(x_{t})
,
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})
includes terms with
I_{(0,0,0),t}
, and
O_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{2})
contains terms such as
I_{(1,1,1,1),t},I_{(0,1,1),t}
. Then, by manipulating the previous equation, we obtain:

\frac{x_{t+\Delta t}-x_{t}}{\Delta t}=f+g\frac{I_{(1),t}}{\Delta t}+L_{0}f\frac{\Delta t}{2}+L_{0}g\frac{I_{(0,1),t}}{\Delta t}\\
+L_{1}f\frac{I_{(1,0),t}}{\Delta t}+L_{1}g\frac{I_{(1,1),t}}{\Delta t}+L_{1}L_{1}g\frac{I_{(1,1,1),t}}{\Delta t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{1})

(254)

Since we assume that
f
can be decomposed in terms of basis functions such that
f(x)=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}b_{i}
, we directly see that our Approximate Maximum Likelihood (AML) estimator
\hat{\alpha}_{i}=G^{-1}_{ij}\left\langle\frac{x_{t+\Delta t}-x_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot b_{j}\right\rangle
can be written as:

\hat{\alpha}_{i}=\alpha_{i}+G^{-1}_{ij}\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left\langle L_{0}fb_{j}\right\rangle+\\
+G^{-1}_{ij}\pqty{ \avg{b_j\pqty{g \frac{I_{(1),t}}{\Delta t} + L_0 g \frac{I_{(0,1),t}}{\Delta t}
+ L_1 f \frac{I_{(1,0),t}}{\Delta t} + L_1 g \frac{I_{(1,1),t}}{\Delta t} + L_1 L_1 g \frac{I_{(1,1,1),t}}{\Delta t}}}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{1})

(255)

From the definition of
I
, all integrals containing stochastic terms
dW_{t}

\left(I_{(1),t},I_{(1,0),t},I_{(0,1),t},I_{(1,1,1),t}\right)
will average to zero. Thus, the bias of
\hat{\alpha}_{i}
is:

\mathbb{E}\left[{\hat{\alpha}_{i}-\alpha_{i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{G^{-1}_{ij}\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left\langle b_{j}L_{0}f\right\rangle}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(256)

For the Mean Squared Error (MSE)
\mathbb{E}\left[{(\hat{\alpha}_{i}-\alpha_{i})^{2}}\right]
, the computation is a bit more involved. To illustrate the calculation, consider the expectation of:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\langle g\frac{I_{(1),t}}{\Delta t}b_{j}\right\rangle\left\langle g\frac{I_{(1),t}}{\Delta t}b_{k}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{t\in\{t_{0},\cdots,\tau\}}\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\{t_{0},\cdots,\tau\}}g(x_{t})\frac{I_{(1),t}}{\Delta t}b_{j}(x_{t})g(x_{t^{\prime}})\frac{I_{(1),t^{\prime}}}{\Delta t}b_{k}(x_{t^{\prime}})}\right]

(257)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Delta tN^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sum_{t\in\{t_{0},\cdots,\tau\}}g(x_{t})^{2}b_{j}(x_{t})b_{k}(x_{t})}\right]

(258)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\langle g^{2}b_{j}b_{k}\right\rangle}\right]

(259)

From this example, we see that all higher-order terms in the MSE coming from products such as
I_{(1),t}I_{(1,0),t}
,
I_{(0,1),t}I_{(1),t}
, and
I_{(1,1,1),t}I_{(1),t}
will be of order
\frac{\Delta t}{\tau}
. By performing some basic algebra and neglecting the correlation between
G_{ij}^{-1}
and
I_{(1),t},I_{(1,0),t},\dots
, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\left[{(\hat{\alpha}_{i}-\alpha_{i})^{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{G_{i,j}^{-1}G_{ik}^{-1}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{\Delta t^{2}}{4}\left\langle b_{j}L_{0}f\right\rangle\left\langle b_{k}L_{0}f\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\tau}\pqty{\avg{g^2 b_j b_k} + \frac{\Delta t}{2}\avg{gL_0g + gL_1f}}}\right]

(260)

Here, the MSE can be decomposed into the squared bias and the variance.
For a long trajectory (
\tau\to\infty
), the variance, being proportional to
\frac{1}{\tau}
, will be negligible with respect to the bias. For a small trajectory, particular attention should be given to both contributions. An idea, not yet explored, is that since all terms in
équation

260
can be estimated, it could be used to determine whether the error primarily stems from the large sampling interval
\Delta t
, the total time of acquiring data, or both.

Appendix 16.B
For the Trapeze-estimator

In this section, we derive the bias and the Mean Squared Error (MSE) of the Trapeze estimator. The goal for this estimator is to be more robust against large sampling intervals than the Approximate Maximum Likelihood (AML) estimator. To achieve this, we look at the Taylor expansion of the drift
f
around
x_{t}
:

f(x_{t+\Delta t})=f+L_{0}fI_{(0),t}+L_{1}fI_{(1),t}+L_{0}L_{0}fI_{(0,0),t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t)

(261)

Then, by using
I_{(0),t}=\Delta t
,
I_{(0,0),t}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta t^{2}
, and
I_{(0),t}I_{(0),t}=2I_{(0,0),t}
, and manipulating the previous equation, we obtain:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=f(x_{t+\Delta t})-L_{0}fI_{(0),t}-L_{1}fI_{(1),t}-L_{0}L_{0}fI_{(0,0),t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t)

\displaystyle\implies f\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}

\displaystyle=f(x_{t+\Delta t})\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}-L_{0}fI_{(0),t}\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}-L_{1}fI_{(1),t}\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}-L_{0}L_{0}fI_{(0,0),t}\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{2})

\displaystyle\implies f\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}

\displaystyle=f(x_{t+\Delta t})\frac{I_{(0),t}}{2}-L_{0}fI_{(0,0),t}-L_{1}f\frac{I_{(0,1),t}+I_{(1,0),t}}{2}-\frac{3}{2}L_{0}L_{0}fI_{(0,0,0),t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{2})

where we used
I_{(0),t}I_{(1),t}=I_{(1,0),t}+I_{(0,1),t}
(
[
60
, p. 172]
). By writing
fI_{(0),t}=\frac{1}{2}fI_{(0),t}+\frac{1}{2}fI_{(0),t}
and injecting the last equation into
équation

253
, we obtain:

x_{t+\Delta t}-x_{t}=\frac{f(x_{t})+f(x_{t+\Delta t})}{2}\Delta t-\frac{1}{2}L_{0}L_{0}fI_{(0,0,0),t}+gI_{(1),t}+L_{1}f\frac{I_{(1,0),t}-I_{(0,1),t}}{2}\\
+L_{0}gI_{(0,1),t}+L_{1}gI_{(1,1),t}+L_{1}L_{1}gI_{(1,1,1),t}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{4})+\mathcal{O}_{\text{fluc}}(\Delta t^{2})

(262)

By assuming that the drift
f
can be decomposed in terms of basis functions (
f(x_{t})=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}b_{i}(x_{t})
), we can derive the inferred parameters
\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{Tr}
(see
équation

138
). By using
équation

138
and
équation

262
, we directly observe that:

\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{Tr}=\alpha_{i}-\frac{\Delta t^{2}}{12}(G^{Tr})^{-1}_{ij}\left\langle b_{j}L_{0}L_{0}f\right\rangle+\\
+\sum_{j}\left(G^{Tr}\right)^{-1}_{ij}\left\langle\frac{b_{j}}{\Delta t}\pqty{g I_{(1),t} + L_1 f \frac{I_{(1,0),t} - I_{(0,1),t}}{2}
+L_0 g I_{(0,1),t} + L_1 g I_{(1,1),t} + L_1 L_1 g I_{(1,1,1),t}}\right\rangle

(263)

By neglecting the correlation between
\left(G^{Tr}\right)^{-1}_{ij}
and the integrals
I_{(0,1),t},I_{(1,0),t},I_{(1,1),t},I_{(1,1,1),t}
in
équation

263
, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\left[{\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{Tr}-\alpha_{i}}\right]=-\frac{\Delta t^{2}}{12}\mathbb{E}\left[{(G^{Tr})^{-1}_{ij}\left\langle b_{j}L_{0}L_{0}f\right\rangle}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3})

(264)

Hence, by using
G^{Tr}
as the Gram matrix instead of
G
in the parameter estimation formula (e.g.,
équation

138
), the bias is reduced from
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)
to
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})
.

To obtain the Mean Squared Error
\mathbb{E}\left[{\pqty{\hat{\alpha}_i^{Tr} - \alpha_i}^{2}}\right]
, we need the following expectations:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta t

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}I_{(0,1),t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta t^{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}I_{(1,0),t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta t^{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}I_{(1,1,1),t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}\left(I_{(1),t}^{3}-3\Delta tI_{(1),t}\right)}\right]=3\Delta t^{2}-3\Delta t^{2}=0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1),t}I_{(1,1),t}}\right]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{I_{(1,1),t}I_{(1,1),t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\pqty{\frac{1}{2}(I_{(1),t}^2-\Delta t)}^{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{4}\pqty{I_{(1),t}^4 - 2 \Delta t I_{(1),t}^2 + \Delta t^2}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\Delta t^{2}

Hence, from
équation

263
, by neglecting the correlation between
G^{Tr}
and the integrals
I_{(0,1),t},I_{(1,0),t},I_{(1,1),t},I_{(1,1,1),t}
, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}\left[{(\hat{\alpha}^{Tr}_{i}-\alpha_{i})^{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{(G^{Tr})_{i,j}^{-1}(G^{Tr})_{ik}^{-1}}\right]\\
\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{\Delta t^{4}}{144}\left\langle b_{j}L_{0}L_{0}f\right\rangle\left\langle b_{k}L_{0}L_{0}f\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\tau}\pqty{\avg{g^2 b_j b_k} + \frac{\Delta t}{2}\avg{gL_0g}}}\right]

(265)

Hence, for a long trajectory, the term proportional to
\frac{1}{\tau}
becomes negligible, and the dominant term is of order
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{4})
, as confirmed by simulation in the main text. Nevertheless, numerical verification of the prefactor was not performed as part of this research due to time constraints.

Chapter 17
Wilks’ Theorem

Appendix 17.A
Introduction

This chapter derives key asymptotic results for Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE) based on a data trajectory
X_{N}=\{x_{1},...,x_{N}\}
of size
N
, when the parameter vector is subject to linear constraints. We first establish the large-sample distribution of the constrained MLE. Subsequently, we derive Wilks’ theorem, which corresponds to the asymptotic distribution of the Likelihood Ratio Test (LRT) statistic used for testing such constraints. These results are fundamental for inference in constrained parameter spaces, particularly when analyzing time series or other sequential data. We will primarily utilize the total Fisher information matrix derived from the full data trajectory
X_{N}
.

Appendix 17.B
Asymptotic Distribution of the Constrained MLE

17.B.1
Model Setup and Reparameterization

Let
\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}):=\ell(\bm{\alpha}\mid X_{N})
be the log-likelihood function based on the data trajectory
X_{N}
, where
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the parameter vector. We consider the case where
\bm{\alpha}
is constrained to lie in a linear subspace defined by a full-rank matrix
\bm{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
, with
r<d
. The constraint is expressed as:

\bm{\alpha}=\bm{H}\bm{\epsilon},

for some reduced parameter vector
\bm{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
.

Under this constraint, the log-likelihood becomes a function of
\bm{\epsilon}
:

\tilde{\ell}_{N}(\bm{\epsilon}):=\ell_{N}(\bm{H}\bm{\epsilon}).

Let
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
denote the MLE of
\bm{\epsilon}
, obtained by maximizing
\tilde{\ell}_{N}(\bm{\epsilon})
. The corresponding constrained MLE for
\bm{\alpha}
is then
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}=\bm{H}\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
. We denote the true parameter values as
\bm{\alpha}_{0}
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{0}
, satisfying
\bm{\alpha}_{0}=\bm{H}\bm{\epsilon}_{0}
.

17.B.2
Asymptotic Distribution of the Reduced Parameter Estimator

Under standard regularity conditions for MLEs (applied to the sequence of log-likelihoods
\ell_{N}
) and assuming model identifiability, the MLE
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
is consistent (
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}\to\bm{\epsilon}_{0}
as
N\to\infty
) and asymptotically normal.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\bm{\alpha})
denote the total Fisher information matrix for the parameter
\bm{\alpha}
based on the sample
X_{N}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}=\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})
be this matrix evaluated at the true parameter value
\bm{\alpha}_{0}
:

\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}}(\bm{\alpha})=-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha})}{\partial\bm{\alpha}\partial\bm{\alpha}^{\top}}\right].

Similarly, let
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}}(\bm{\epsilon})
be the total Fisher information matrix for the reduced parameter
\bm{\epsilon}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}=\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}}(\bm{\epsilon}_{0})
:

\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}}(\bm{\epsilon})=-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{\ell}_{N}(\bm{\epsilon})}{\partial\bm{\epsilon}\partial\bm{\epsilon}^{\top}}\right].

Using the chain rule (
\frac{\partial^{2}\tilde{\ell}_{N}}{\partial\bm{\epsilon}\partial\bm{\epsilon}^{\top}}=\bm{H}^{\top}\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{N}}{\partial\bm{\alpha}\partial\bm{\alpha}^{\top}}\bm{H}
), we find the relationship between the information matrices evaluated at the true values:

\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}=\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H}.

The asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
can be expressed using this total information. For large
N
, the distribution of the estimator around the true value is approximately Gaussian:

(\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}-\bm{\epsilon}_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{-1}\right).

More formally,
\sqrt{N}(\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}-\bm{\epsilon}_{0})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,\lim_{N\to\infty}[N\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{-1}])
(assuming
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}
is
O(N)
and the limit of
N\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{-1}
exists, or alternatively, if
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{*}
is the average Fisher information, then
\sqrt{N}(\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}-\bm{\epsilon}_{0})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,(\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{*})^{-1})
). However, working with the total information
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}
directly (as defined) simplifies many expressions for the non-scaled estimator.

17.B.3
Asymptotic Distribution of the Constrained Estimator

The constrained estimator
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}
is a linear transformation of
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
:

\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}=\bm{H}\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}.

Using the large-sample approximate distribution for
\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
, we find the approximate distribution of
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}
:

(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})=\bm{H}(\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}-\bm{\epsilon}_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\bm{H}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\right).

Substituting the expression for
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\epsilon}_{0}}
, we obtain that the distribution of the constrained estimator around the true value is:

\boxed{(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\right).}

Appendix 17.C
Wilks’ Theorem: Asymptotic Distribution of the Likelihood Ratio Statistic

We now derive the asymptotic distribution of the LRT statistic for testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}:\bm{\alpha}=\bm{H}\bm{\epsilon}
for some
\bm{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
against the alternative hypothesis
H_{A}:\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

17.C.1
Estimators and Log-likelihood Expansion

Let
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u}
be the unconstrained MLE maximizing
\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha})
over
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}=\bm{H}\hat{\bm{\epsilon}}
be the constrained MLE, as defined previously.

We use a second-order Taylor expansion of
\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha})
around the true parameter
\bm{\alpha}_{0}
:

\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha})\approx\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+(\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\nabla^{2}\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})(\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\alpha}_{0}).

Under standard regularity conditions for large
N
:

•

The Hessian approximates the negative total Fisher information:
\nabla^{2}\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})\approx-\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}
.

•

The score vector at the true parameter is asymptotically normal:
\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}})
.

•

The estimators relate to the score:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u}-\bm{\alpha}_{0}

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}-\bm{\alpha}_{0}

\displaystyle\approx\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})

Substituting these approximations back into the Taylor expansion for
\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha})
around
\bm{\alpha}_{0}
, and using
\nabla^{2}\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})\approx-\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}
, we get:

\displaystyle\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u})

\displaystyle\approx\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u}-\bm{\alpha}_{0})

\displaystyle\approx\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})

\displaystyle\approx\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0}).

Similarly, for the constrained estimator:

\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c})\approx\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0}).

17.C.2
Difference in Log-Likelihoods

The difference between the maximized log-likelihoods under the unconstrained and constrained models is approximately:

\displaystyle\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u})-\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}-\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\right]\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0}).

17.C.3
Likelihood Ratio Statistic and its Distribution

The Likelihood Ratio (LR) statistic is defined as:

\lambda=2\left[\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u})-\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c})\right].

Substituting the approximation for the difference:

\lambda\approx\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})^{\top}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}-\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\right]\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0}).

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}
be a matrix square root of
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}
. We know that
\bm{Z}=\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1/2}\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})
is asymptotically
\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d})
, where
\bm{I}_{d}
is the
d\times d
identity matrix. We can rewrite
\nabla\ell_{N}(\bm{\alpha}_{0})=\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{Z}
. Substituting this into the expression for
\lambda
:

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\approx\bm{Z}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{-1}-\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\right]\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{Z}

\displaystyle\approx\bm{Z}^{\top}\left[\bm{I}_{d}-\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\right]\bm{Z}.

Let
\bm{P}=\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}
. This matrix
\bm{P}
is a projection matrix onto the subspace spanned by the columns of
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{H}
. Thus, we have
\text{rank}(P)=\text{rank}(\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{H})=\text{rank}(\bm{H})=r
, because
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}
is full rank. Then, the expression simplifies to:

\lambda\approx\bm{Z}^{\top}(\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P})\bm{Z}.

The matrix
\bm{I}_{d}-\bm{P}
is also a projection matrix, projecting onto the orthogonal complement of the subspace spanned by the columns of
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}^{1/2}\bm{H}
. Its rank is
d-\text{rank}(\bm{P})=d-r
.

By Cochran’s theorem, a quadratic form
\bm{Z}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{Z}
where
\bm{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{I})
and
\bm{A}
is an idempotent matrix (projection) follows a chi-squared distribution with degrees of freedom equal to the rank of
\bm{A}
.
Therefore,

\boxed{\lambda=2\left[\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u})-\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c})\right]\xrightarrow{d}\chi^{2}_{d-r}.}

where
d-r
can be seen as the number of independent linear constraints imposed by the null hypothesis.

This demonstration was inspired by the methods presented in lecture notes from Charles J. Geyer available at
https://www.stat.umn.edu/geyer/8112/notes/tests.pdf
.

Appendix 17.D
Summary

We have derived two key asymptotic results for MLE under the linear constraint
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{H}\bm{\epsilon}
:

1.

The constrained MLE
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c}
is asymptotically normal with mean
\bm{\alpha}_{0}
and covariance matrix
\bm{H}(\bm{H}^{\top}\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}\bm{H})^{-1}\bm{H}^{\top}
. (This assumes
\mathcal{I}_{\bm{\alpha}_{0}}
is the total Fisher information, consistent with the boxed formula earlier. The original had a
1/n
factor here which seemed inconsistent.)

2.

The Likelihood Ratio Test statistic
\lambda=2[\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{u})-\ell_{N}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}_{c})]
for testing the validity of the constraint asymptotically follows a chi-squared distribution with
d-r
degrees of freedom, where
d-r
is the number of independent linear restrictions imposed by the constraint. This is also known as Wilks’ theorem.

These results allow for constructing confidence intervals and performing hypothesis tests regarding the linear constraints.

Chapter 18
Mathematical Derivations and Simulation Details for Part III

This appendix provides supplementary mathematical details for the derivations presented in
partie

III
and lists the parameters used in the benchmark simulations.

Appendix 18.A
Estimating the Error
\mathcal{E}
from the Log-Likelihood

We aim to prove Eq. (
178
), which connects the inference error
\mathcal{E}(\bm{\hat{f}}^{\mathcal{B}})
to the estimated log-likelihood
\ell(\bm{\hat{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}\mid\bm{X}_{\tau})
. For simplicity, we assume the estimated diffusion
\bm{\bar{D}}
equals the true (potentially averaged) diffusion matrix
\bm{D}
. We also assume
\Delta t
is small enough to justify the Euler-Maruyama approximation
\Delta\bm{x}_{t}\approx\bm{f}(\bm{x}_{t})\Delta t+\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}
, where
\Delta\bm{\Xi_{t}}=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\sqrt{2\bm{D}(\bm{x}_{s})}\bm{\xi}(s)\differential{s}
.

Expanding the negative log-likelihood (Eq. (
175
)):

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\tau}\ell\left(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\frac{\Delta\bm{x}_{t}}{\Delta t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right)\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\left(\frac{\Delta\bm{x}_{t}}{\Delta t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\approx\left\langle\left((\bm{f}_{t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t})+\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right)\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\left((\bm{f}_{t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t})+\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left\langle(\bm{f}_{t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot(\bm{f}_{t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t})\right\rangle}_{4\mathcal{E}(\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}})}+2\left\langle(\bm{f}_{t}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left\langle\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}_{C^{\prime}}

The term
C^{\prime}
involves only the noise increments and is independent of the model
\mathcal{B}
, thus irrelevant for model comparison. Let
C=\tau C^{\prime}/4
. We focus on the cross term. Using the Itô isometry property
\mathbb{E}\left[{\Delta\Xi_{t,\alpha}\Delta\Xi_{t,\beta}}\right]=2D_{\alpha\beta}(\bm{x}_{t})\Delta t+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})
and the fact that
\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}
is independent of past states (including
\bm{f}_{t}
), we have
\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\langle\bm{f}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}\right]=0
.

However, the term involving the inferred force
\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
requires care because
\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
depends on the entire trajectory, including future noise increments, through the coefficient calculation Eq. (
176
). Let
\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}=\sum_{i}\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{\mathcal{B}}\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{-2\left\langle\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{-2\sum_{i}\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{\mathcal{B}}\left\langle\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{-2\sum_{i,j}\left(G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\right)_{ij}\left\langle\frac{\Delta\bm{x}_{s}}{\Delta s}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\bm{b_{j}}(\bm{x}_{s})\right\rangle\left\langle\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}\right]

Substituting
\Delta\bm{x}_{s}\approx\bm{f}_{s}\Delta s+\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{s}
and keeping the leading terms related to noise correlations (neglecting correlations between
G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}
and noise, which yield higher-order terms):
The dominant contribution comes from
s=t
:

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{-2\sum_{i,j}\left(G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\right)_{ij}\left\langle\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\bm{b_{j}}(\bm{x}_{t})\right\rangle\left\langle\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{-\frac{2}{\tau}\sum_{i,j}\left(G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\right)_{ij}\sum_{t}\left(\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\bm{b_{j}}(\bm{x}_{t})\right)\left(\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\Delta\bm{\Xi}_{t}}{\Delta t}\right)\Delta t}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}\left[{-\frac{4}{\tau\Delta t}\sum_{i,j}\left(G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\right)_{ij}\sum_{t}\bm{b}_{i}(\bm{x}_{t})\cdot\mathbf{D}^{-1}\cdot\bm{b_{j}}(\bm{x}_{t})\Delta t}\right]\quad\text{(Using Itô isometry properties)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{-\frac{4}{\tau}\sum_{i,j}\left(G_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\right)_{ij}(G_{\mathcal{B}})_{ji}}\right]=-\frac{4n_{\mathcal{B}}}{\tau}.

Thus, taking the expectation of the negative log-likelihood expansion:

\mathbb{E}\left[{-\frac{4}{\tau}\ell\left(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}\right)}\right]\approx 4\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{E}(\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}})}\right]-\frac{4n_{\mathcal{B}}}{\tau}+C^{\prime}

Multiplying by
-\tau/4
:

\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell\left(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}}\right)}\right]\approx-\tau\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{E}(\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}})}\right]+n_{\mathcal{B}}-C

(266)

Taking the difference between two models
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
cancels the constant
C
and yields Eq. (
178
):

\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell(\mathcal{C})-\ell(\mathcal{B})}\right]\approx\tau\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{E}(\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{B}})-\mathcal{E}(\hat{\bm{f}}^{\mathcal{C}})}\right]+n_{\mathcal{C}}-n_{\mathcal{B}}.

Appendix 18.B
Derivation of the Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC)

We derive the BIC formula
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{BIC}}(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{B})-\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log(\tau)
presented in
équation

186
. BIC approximates the log marginal likelihood (or Bayesian evidence) for a model
\mathcal{B}
. The marginal likelihood is obtained by integrating the likelihood over the prior distribution of the parameters
\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}=\{\alpha_{1}^{\mathcal{B}},\dots,\alpha_{n_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\mathcal{B}}\}
:

P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\mathcal{B})=\int P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{B})\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})\differential{\bm{\alpha}^\mathcal{B}}

(267)

where
P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{B})=\frac{1}{Z}e^{\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})}
is the likelihood (
Z
is a normalization constant independent of
\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}
) and
\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})
is the prior.

We use Laplace’s approximation for the integral. We Taylor-expand the log-likelihood
\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})
around its maximum, which occurs at the inferred parameters
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}}
:

\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})\approx\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})+(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{T}\nabla\ell+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{T}\nabla^{2}\ell(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})

At the maximum,
\nabla\ell=0
. The Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}\ell
can be calculated from Eq. (
175
) (assuming parameters are
F_{k}^{\mathcal{B}}=\alpha_{k}^{\mathcal{B}}
):

\frac{\partial^{2}\ell}{\partial\alpha_{i}^{\mathcal{B}}\partial\alpha_{j}^{\mathcal{B}}}=-\frac{\tau}{2}\left\langle\bm{b}_{i}\cdot\bar{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\cdot\bm{b}_{j}\right\rangle=-\frac{\tau}{2}(G_{\mathcal{B}})_{ij}
. So,
\nabla^{2}\ell=-\frac{\tau}{2}\bm{G}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})\approx\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})-\frac{\tau}{4}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{T}\bm{G}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})

Substituting this into the integral and assuming the prior
\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}})
is approximately constant around
\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}}
:

\displaystyle P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{Z}e^{\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})}\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})\int e^{-\frac{\tau}{4}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})^{T}\bm{G}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{\alpha}^{\mathcal{B}}-\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})}\differential{\bm{\alpha}^\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}e^{\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})}\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{n_{\mathcal{B}}}}{\det(\frac{\tau}{2} \bm{G}_\mathcal{B})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})}}{Z}\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})\left(\frac{4\pi}{\tau}\right)^{\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}}(\det\bm{G}_{\mathcal{B}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

Taking the logarithm:

\log P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\mathcal{B})\approx\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})+\log\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})-\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log\tau+\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log(4\pi)-\frac{1}{2}\log(\det\bm{G}_\mathcal{B})-\log Z

For large
\tau
, the dominant term involving
n_{\mathcal{B}}
is
-\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log\tau
. The terms involving the prior and the determinant of the Gram matrix are typically
\mathcal{O}(1)
or grow slower than
\log\tau
. Neglecting constant terms and terms that do not scale with
n_{\mathcal{B}}\log\tau
, we obtain the BIC approximation:

\log P(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\mathcal{B})\approx\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})-\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log\tau

Since
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{B})=\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|\hat{\bm{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{B}})-\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|0)
, maximizing BIC is equivalent to maximizing
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{BIC}}(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{B})-\frac{n_{\mathcal{B}}}{2}\log(\tau)
, ignoring the model-independent
\ell(\bm{X}_{\tau}|0)
. The use of total time
\tau
instead of the number of data points
N=\tau/\Delta t
arises naturally from the SDE likelihood formulation and is consistent with
[
53
]
.

Appendix 18.C
Simulations Details and Parameters

All simulations use the Euler–Maruyama method with simulation time step
\differential{t}
, sampling interval
\Delta t
(with
\Delta t=10\differential{t}
), and total time
\tau
. For the prediction error
section

48.2
, the independent test trajectories have duration
\tau_{\mathcal{E}}
. In all cases, initial conditions are taken after a thermalization period
\tau_{\text{therm.}}=10
. Results are averaged over 100 simulations.

•

Lorenz System
(
figures

17
,
19
,
23
et
24
): The system parameters are
\sigma=10,\rho=28,\beta=7/3

figure

18
. The diffusion constant is
D=100
, the sampling interval
\Delta t=2\times 10^{-4}
, and
\tau_{\mathcal{E}}=20
. Specifically for
figures

24
et
23
, we use
\tau=4\times 10^{3}
. The total library
\mathcal{B}_{0}
contains monomials up to order 2 in
x,y,z
for each component (
n_{0}=3\times 10=30
). The true model
\mathcal{B}^{*}
has
n^{*}=7
basis functions.

•

Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
(
figures

17
,
19
,
23
et
24
):
\derivative{\bm{x}}{t}=-\bm{A}\bm{x}+\sqrt{2\bm{D}}\bm{\xi}(t)
. Dimension
d=10
with
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Matrix
\bm{A}
has diagonal elements equal to 1 (
A_{ii}=1
), and 10% of off-diagonal elements are randomly set to
\pm 1
, with other elements being 0. Thus, the true model has
n^{*}=d+0.1d(d-1)\approx 19
parameters. The simulation details are
\bm{D}=100\bm{I}
,
\differential{t}=0.001
,
\Delta t=0.01
,
\tau_{\mathcal{E}}=10^{3}
. The total library
\mathcal{B}_{0}
:
\{\bm{e_{j}}\}_{j=1\dots d}\cup\{x_{k}\bm{e}_{j}\}_{j,k=1..d}
has
n_{0}=d+d^{2}=110
functions.

•

Lotka-Volterra
(
figures

17
,
19
,
23
et
24
):
\derivative{x_i}{t}=x_{i}(r_{i}+\sum_{j}A_{ij}x_{j})+\sqrt{2Dx_{i}^{2}}\xi_{i}(t)
with dimension
d=10
,
r_{i}=1
and
D=0.05
. The interaction matrix has diagonal terms equal to
-1
(
A_{ii}=-1
), and the off-diagonal
A_{ij}
are sparse with
\pm 1
values as in
figure

18
and
section

17.2.1
. For the simulations, we use
\differential{t}=0.001
,
\Delta t=0.01
,
\tau_{\mathcal{E}}=100
. The total basis is presented in
figure

18
and
section

17.2.1
.

•

Gray-Scott
(
figures

21
et
22
): The Gray-Scott model is composed of 2 SPDEs:
\partialderivative{u}{t}=D_{u}\nabla^{2}u-uv^{2}+F(1-u)+\sqrt{2D}\xi_{u}
and
\partialderivative{v}{t}=D_{v}\nabla^{2}v+uv^{2}-(F+k)v+\sqrt{2D}\xi_{v}
. The parameters are
D_{u}=0.2097,D_{v}=0.105,F=0.029,k=0.057
, and
D=0.001
. For the simulations, we used a square lattice
100\times 100
(
L_{x}=L_{y}=100
) with periodic boundaries,
\Delta x=\Delta y=1
, with simulation time step
\differential{t}=0.001
, and sampling interval of the data
\Delta t=0.01
. For the prediction error, we use independent trajectories of duration
\tau_{\mathcal{E}}=10
. The total library is presented in
figure

18
.

Appendix 18.D
Benchmarking and Hyperparameter Tuning for Comparison Methods

To ensure a fair comparison between PASTIS and existing methods (SINDy, k-fold CV, LASSO), we performed careful hyperparameter tuning for these comparison methods.
figure

25
consolidates the results, showing performance metrics versus the primary hyperparameter for each method across the four benchmark systems. The metrics shown are Exact Match (blue), True Positives (TP, green), False Positives (FP, yellow), and False Negatives (FN, orange), defined as:

\text{TP}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{B}^{*}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{B}^{*}\rvert},\qquad\text{FP}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{B}^{*}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{B}^{*}\rvert},\qquad\text{FN}=\frac{\lvert\mathcal{B}^{*}-\mathcal{B}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{B}^{*}\rvert},

(268)

where
\mathcal{B}
is the model selected by the algorithm and
\mathcal{B}^{*}
is the true model.

Figure 25:
Comparison of model selection performance versus hyperparameters for PASTIS, SINDy, k-fold CV, and LASSO across four benchmark systems (columns: Lorenz D=0.01, Lorenz D=100, Ornstein-Uhlenbeck, Lotka-Volterra). Each row corresponds to a method, plotting performance metrics against its primary hyperparameter:
p
for PASTIS (row 1, panels a1-d1), STLSQ threshold
\lambda
for SINDy (row 2, panels a2-d2), number of folds
k
for k-fold CV (row 3, panels a3-d3), and regularization strength
\alpha
for LASSO (row 4, panels a4-d4). Performance metrics are Exact Match (blue), True Positives (TP, green), False Positives (FP, yellow), and False Negatives (FN, orange). The vertical dashed lines indicate default or chosen parameter values used in main text comparisons where applicable (e.g.,
p=0.001
for PASTIS,
\lambda=0.5
for SINDy). Note that the x-axis scale and range vary between rows depending on the relevant hyperparameter.

18.D.1
SINDy (STLSQ)

The performance of SINDy depends significantly on its STLSQ threshold hyperparameter
\lambda
, as shown in the second row of
figure

25
(panels a2-d2). The optimal
\lambda
varies with the system and noise level. For low noise (Lorenz D=0.01, panel a2), a sharp threshold exists where the exact match rate is high. However, for noisier systems (panels b2-d2), this performance window disappears. Even near-optimal
\lambda
values (e.g.,
\lambda\approx 0.5-1.0
for Lorenz D=100,
\lambda\approx 0.1-0.5
for OU and LV), SINDy often selects models with substantial numbers of both false positives (FP, yellow lines) and false negatives (FN, orange lines). This highlights its sensitivity and difficulty in achieving exact sparse recovery in challenging stochastic settings. The value
\lambda=0.5
used in the main text represents a reasonable choice across the noisy systems tested.

18.D.2
k-Fold Cross-Validation (CV)

The third row of
figure

25
(panels a3-d3) shows the performance of k-fold CV versus the number of folds
k
. The results are largely insensitive to
k
in the range 2-10. More importantly, k-fold CV consistently fails to achieve a high exact match rate for any
k
across all systems. It primarily suffers from a high rate of false positives (FP, yellow lines), indicating that, like AIC, it does not sufficiently penalize model complexity when selecting from a large library in this SDE context.

18.D.3
LASSO

The fourth row of
figure

25
(panels a4-d4) shows the performance of LASSO versus its regularization parameter
\alpha
. Similar to SINDy, performance is sensitive to
\alpha
. However, across the tested range, LASSO failed to achieve a high exact match rate for any of the stochastic systems. It typically exhibits a trade-off where reducing false positives (by increasing
\alpha
) simultaneously increases false negatives, failing to identify the correct sparse structure. Its overall performance was significantly worse than PASTIS (row 1) and often worse than SINDy.

18.D.4
Conclusion

Based on these comprehensive hyperparameter analyses presented in
figure

25
, the comparisons in the main text (
figures

17
,
19
,
23
et
24
) are well-justified, using representative or near-optimal parameters for the benchmark methods. The figure clearly demonstrates the superior robustness and accuracy of PASTIS in achieving exact model recovery across different systems compared to SINDy, k-fold CV, and LASSO, especially when considering the challenge of minimizing both false positives and false negatives simultaneously.
